CHAPTER XXXII

Yoga and Dharmaśāstra

[[P1385]]Sāṅkhya and Yoga often go together in the Upaniṣads [[28|as]] well as in the Mahābhārata, the [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] and the Purāṇas and their relationship to one another is the same in all these. In [[Sv.|Śv.]] Up. VI. 13, Vanaparva 2. 15, [[śāntiparva|Śāntiparva]]1 [[228, 28,|228. 28,]] [[289.1,|289. 1,]] 306. 65, [[308, 25,|308. 25,]] 326. 100, 336. 69, Anuśāsana 14. 323, [[Bhagavad gitā|Bhagavadgītā]] V. [[4-5,|4–5,]] Padma-[[purāpa|purāṇa]] ([[Pātālakbanda,|Pātālakhaṇḍa,]] 85. 11 ff), the two are mentioned [[together, |together.]]

Though [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] greatly influenced all works dealing with the evolution of the world in its various [[aspecte,|aspects,]] it did not enjoy that great esteem among all sorts of people in India that the Yoga system enjoyed and still enjoys. The word yoga is derived from the root ‘yuj’ to join or to unite (of rudhādi class). The germs of yoga can be traced back to the [[Rgveda.|Ṛgveda.]] [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] V. 81. 1 [[( a |(a ]]verse in praise of [[Savitp )|Savitṛ)]] reads ‘wise men, priests and sacrificers concentrate their minds and join their prayers to the wise, the great (Savitṛ), who knows all (prayers)’. Another [[Vedic -271|Vedic]]2 verse also speaks of concentrating the mind. The word ‘yoga’ occurs frequently in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] in several shades of meaning. [[Sāyana|Sāyaṇa]] in many passages takes ‘yoga’ to mean ‘acquiring what is not already possessed’ (as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 5. 3). In [[Rg,|Ṛg.]] I. 18. 7 God Sadasaspati (Agni) is said to pervade the prayers (or thoughts) of sacrificers. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 34. 9 it means ‘yoking’ ([[‘kada|(‘kadā]] yogo vājino [[rāsabhagya|rāsabhasya]] yena yajñam [[Bāsatyo payāthaḥ’.|Nāsatyopayāthaḥ’).]] The word ‘yoga’ is often used with the word kṣema (separately as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VII. 54. 3, VII. 86. 8) or as a compound

[[P1386]](as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 166. 5 ‘Yogakṣemam va [[adayaham bbūyāsam uttamal’).|ādāyāhaṁ bhūyāsamuttamah’).]] There is a great distance or gap between the meaning of the word yoga in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] (even in the verses about concentrating the mind) and its meaning in some of the Upaniṣads and in classical Sanskrit. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 136. [[2-3 thero|2–3 there]] is [[mention 3272|mention]]3 of munis, [[eons|sons]] of [[Vātarajada,|Vātaraśana,]] who wore dirty and tawny garments and who say in verse 3 ‘we being in [[oostasy|ecstasy]] (or wild bliss) owing to [[cur|our]] way of life as munis resort to winds; mortals! you look on our bodies only’. This shows that even in those ancient times, some people practised tapas, did not care what clothes they wore and thought they (i.e. their souls) would be merged in the wind (that is, the souls are formless and could not be seen). In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 17. 14 Indra is said to be a friend of munis and muni is also a friend of every god in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 136. 4. But as to yatis the position was rather different. The word [[yali|yati]] occurs several times in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] but in most cases that word has no certain connection with the meaning of [[‘ascetio’.|‘ascetic’.]] In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 3. 9 ‘by which (says [[brabma|brahmā]] priest) wealth was given to [[Börgu|Bhṛgu]] from the yatis and by which you helped (or protected) [[Praskanva’;|Praskaṇva’;]] here Indra [[sooms|seems]] to be opposed to yatis. In [[Rg. VIII, 6. 18|Ṛg. VIII. 6. 18]] the poet says ‘O valiant Indra! listen to my prayer alone from among the yatis and Bhṛgus that praised [[thee |thee’.]] Here Sāyaṇa explains ‘yatayaḥ’ as [[‘Angirasah’.|‘Aṅgirasaḥ’.]] In any case ‘yatis’ are here shown to be devotees of Indra. But in other [[Sarbitas|Saṁhitās]] it is said that Indra threw ‘yatis’ to the wolves or [[hyenas, 1773|hyenas.]]4 Later on, however, the meaning of yati seems to have changed. In those Samhitā passages ‘yatis’ appear to be persons hostile or alien to the Vedic rites, but what they did to deserve

[[P1387]]slaughter by Indra is not clear. In Atharvaveda II. 5. 3 Indra is said to have killed Vṛtra as he did in the case of yatis. Some of the Upaniṣads, however, show that ‘yati’ was a person who had given up worldly affairs, practised yoga and endeavoured after the knowledge of Ātman and realized Brahma; vide Muṇḍaka Up. III. 1. 5 (yaṁ paśyanti yatayaḥ kṣīṇadoṣāḥ) and III. 2. 6 (sannyāsayogād-yatayaḥ śuddhasattvāḥ). Some like Haüer in ‘Die [[Anfrange|Anfänge]] der Yoga-praxis’, 1922 pp. 11 ff) hold that the Vrātyas of Atharvaveda XV were ecstatics of the kṣatriya class and forerunners of Yogins.

The word ‘yoga’ occurs in some of the Upaniṣads in the same sense in which it is employed in the Yogasūtra. In Kaṭha Up. II. [[1%,|12,]] it is said5] ’the wise man reflecting on God by means of yoga, by concentrating the mind on the inner spirit becomes free from joy and grief’ ([[adhyātmayogadhigamona|adhyātmayogādhigamena]]). The same Upaniṣad says that the state described in VI. 11 is regarded as Yoga because therein the organs (and the mind and buddhi) are firmly held under control. [[Ia|In]] Kaṭha Up. [[VL.|VI.]] 18 it is said that Naciketas having understood the vidyā and the whole procedure of Yoga propounded by Yama attained (the realization of) brahma. The word ‘Yoga’ occurs in the Tai. Up. II. 4 (in speaking about vijñānamaya ātman, yoga is said to be its ātman, the exact sense being doubtful), and in the Śv. Up. II. 11 and VI. 13. [[Praśna (5.5-6)|Praśna (5. 5–6)]] speaks of the three mātrās (a, [[u,m|u, m)]] of Om. In Śv. Up. [[1. 3 Occurs|I. 3 occurs]] the word ‘dhyānayoga’. The Śv. Up. (II. 8–13) refers to āsana and [[prāpāyāma|prāṇāyāma]] and sets out the first signs of the successful [[practics|practice]] of Yoga. The Chān. Up. VIII. 15 appears to refer to pratyāhāra (though the technical word is not [[used )|used)]] in [[‘atmani|‘ātmani]] sarvendriyāṇi [[pratiṣthāpya’(having|pratiṣṭhāpya’ (having]] brought all organs of sense to rest in the [[atman).’|ātman).’]] The [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. ([[1.5.23|I. 5. 23]]) appears to refer to prāṇāyāma in ‘he should observe the one vow, viz. inhalation and [[exhala tion|exhalation]] ([[tasmad-ekameva|tasmād-ekameva]] vratam [[carot prāṇyao-oaiva apanyacca).|caret prāṇyāc-caivāpānyācca)’.]]

[[P1388]]The [[Vedantasūtra|Vedāntasūtra]] (II. 1. 3 ’etena yogaḥ [[pratyuktah)|pratyuktaḥ’)]] states that Yoga is refuted by the reasoning adopted for the refutation of the Sāṅkhya system. The position of [[Saskarāoārga|Śaṅkarācārya]] as to both [[Sāokhya|Sāṅkhya]] and Yoga has been stated above (pp. 1352 and 1361 n. 2208 and 2221). He points out in the pūrvapakṣa that [[Voda 2375|Veda]]6 [[proscribes|prescribes]] Yoga as a means of achieving correct knowledge and refers to Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 5. He further points out that in the Śv. Up. Yoga is expounded in great detail after first mentioning the proper posture (for the practice of Yoga) in the words ‘having held the body [[straigbt|straight]] but raised high in three places viz. chest, neck and head’ (Śv. Up. II. 8). From the words of Śaṅkarācārya that in Yogaśāstra also Yoga is held to be a means of the realisation of right knowledge, it follows that [[be|he]] had a yogaśāstra before him in which occurred the words ‘atha…[[yogah,’|yogaḥ’,]] but as he does not [[einploy|employ]] the word Yogasūtra he probably does not refer to a sūtra work. If a conjecture may be hazarded, it is possible that the Yogaśāstra meant by [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] is a work like the Yogaśāstra ascribed to Yājñavalkya in Yāj. [[Smrti|Smṛti]] III. 110 ([[Yogaśāstram ca mat-proktam &c.).|Yogaśāstraṁ ca mat-proktaṁ &c.).]] [[Śhaṅkaracārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] admits on V. S. [[]], 1, 3|II. 1. 3]] that a part of the yoga is acceptable to him but other parts conflict with the Veda. Muṇḍaka Up. ([[II 2.6|II. 2. 6]]) enjoins [[samūdhi|samādhi]] in the words ‘om-iti dhyāyatha ātmānam’ as Śaṅkarācārya states on V. S. II. 3. 39 [[(samadhyabhāvāc-ca).|(samādhyabhāvāc-ca).]] In the [[Upanisada|Upaniṣads]] ‘Muni’ and ‘Yati’ have come to [[denote 2276|denote]]7 the same meaning, [[o g.|e.g.]] Bṛ. Up. IV. 4. 22 states ‘one becomes muni by realizing this very self,’

[[P1389]]while the Muṇḍaka (III. 1. [[5) 2777|5)]]8 states ’this self is to be attained by Truth, by tapas, by right knowledge and by sexual purity at all times: that self resides inside the body (like a light), is pure, whom spotless ascetics realize.’ The [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] (III. [[13),|13)]] provides that the wise man should restrain speech in mind ([[ speech| (speech]] and mind, as the text stands), he should keep it within the self which is knowledge, he should keep down knowledge within the self that [[is,|is]] the great, and [[he,|he]] should keep that (the great) within the self which is quiet. The [[Upaniṣada|Upaniṣads]] thus not only employ the word ‘Yoga’ but provide some of the stages of yoga and its technique for [[realiz ing|realizing]] the Supreme Spirit. About twenty Yoga Upaniṣads are published at Adyar, edited by A. Mahadev Sastri (1920), but as their chronology is most uncertain and as most of them appear to the present writer to be later than the Mahābhārata, Manu and probably the Yogasūtra, they are passed over here and are referred to in this work sparingly.9

Pāṇini provides for the formation of the words yama and niyama (two [[angas)|aṅgas)]] of Yoga and the word ‘Yogin’ appears to have been derived by him from the root ‘yuj’ with the affix [[‘ghinun’(i.|‘ghinun’ (i.]] e. in) in the sense of [[tūcchīlya|tācchīlya]] (being [[babituated|habituated]] to) according to Pāṇ. III. 2. 142 (which is a very long sūtra).10]

[[P1390]]The [[Apastambadharmasūtra|Āpastambadharmasūtra]] (I. 8. 23. 3–6) quotes a verse ‘The total destruction of doṣas (taints) is due to Yoga in this [[life ;|life;]] a wise man after having expelled all doṣas that cause harm to all beings reaches peace ([[moksa|mokṣa]])’ and then it sets out fifteen doṣas such as anger, ecstatic joy, greed, hypocrisy, the destruction of which is due to Yoga and enumerates the qualities that are opposites of the doṣas. This shows that long before the 4th or 5th century B. C. Yoga as a discipline of the mind had been well developed.

The V. S. II. 1. 3 indicates that the Sūtrakāra had before him a body of Yoga doctrines, some of which were the same as those of the Sāṅkhya and he also knew Samādhi (V. S. II. 3. 39). Again, the V. S. mentions (in IV. 2. 21) Yogins and distinguishes Sāṅkhya and Yoga as smārta (and not śrauta). Śaṅkarācārya on V. S. I. 3. 33 quotes the extant Yogasūtra II. 44 (svādhyāyādiṣṭa-devatā-samprayogaḥ) and [[on 2280|on]]11 V. S. II. 4. 12 he appears to be willing to admit that the Yoga-sūtra preceded V. S. and quotes Yogasūtra I. 6 in the 2nd interpretation of that sūtra.

The important question is whether the author of the V. S. refers to the extant Yogasūtra. The present author holds for various reasons that the V. S. does not refer to the present Yoga-sūtra, but to the Yoga doctrines that had been developed even before the Kaṭha, Muṇḍaka, Śvetāśvatara and other Upaniṣads.

In the Śāntiparva it is stated that the [[propounder 2281|propounder]]12 of Sāṅkhya was paramarṣi (highest sage) Kapila, [[Hiranyagarbba|Hiraṇyagarbha]]

[[P1391]]was the ancient knower of Yoga, no one else (knew it); Apāntaratamas was Vedācārya whom some called the sage [[Prāclnagarbha.|Prācīnagarbha.]] In the previous chapter it is stated that Sāṅkhya, Yoga, Vedāraṇyaka and Pañcarātra are one and are [[angas|aṅgas]] of each other. In Śānti. 326. 65 Hiraṇyagarbha is again connected with Yogaśāstra. Vide p. 1371 above. In the [[Anuśāsanaparva 2282|Anuśāsanaparva]]13 Sanatkumāra appears to be connected with Yoga as the founder, just as Kapila was the founder of Sāṅkhya. In the Ahirbudhnya Saṁhitā (XII. [[32-33|32–33]]) it is stated that Hiraṇyagarbha first propounded two Yogasaṁhitās, one was called ‘Nirodhayoga’ and the other was called ‘Karmayoga’, the first of which was again divided twelve-fold. The [[Bhāmat! 2283|Bhāmatī]]14 on V. S. II. 1. 3 states that that sūtra does not totally negative the authoritativeness of the Yogaśāstra of Hiraṇyagarbha and Patañjali. The Viṣṇu-[[purāpa 2284|purāṇa]]15 appears to quote two verses from Hiraṇyagarbha (vide note below). Vācaspati in his commentary on Yogasūtra I. 1 states that Yogi-Yājñavalkya mentions that Hiraṇyagarbha was the propounder of Yoga. Vācaspati regards the Yogasūtra of Patañjali as later than [[Yoga-Yājñavalkya-smrti.|Yoga-Yājñavalkya-smṛti.]] Therefore, it is almost certain that the V. S. refutes the doctrines of that Yoga system that was known to the Śāntiparva.

In Śalyaparva (chap. 50) a story is told about bhikṣu [[Jaiglṣavya|Jaigīṣavya]] who was a great Yogin and of ‘Asita Devala’, a householder staying on the holy Sārasvata-tīrtha.

In the Śāntiparva (cr. ed. chap. 222 = Ch. ed. chap. 229) there is a long dialogue between Jaigīṣavya and Asita about Yoga, one verse [[(14) 2285|(14)]]16 from which may be cited

[[P1392]]here ‘Yogins do not talk much by way of censure and praise of others and their minds are never affected by the praise and censure of them indulged in by others.’ In that chap. [[Jaigtṣavya|Jaigīṣavya]] is described as one who was never angry nor joyful. In the Varāhapurāṇa (4. 14) it is stated that Kapila and [[Yogirat Jaiglṣarya|Yogirāṭ Jaigīṣavya]] came to king Aśvaśiras that had taken the avabhṛtha [[hath|bath]] after Aśvamedha and assumed the forms of Viṣṇu and Garuḍa respectively. It may be noted that on Y. S. II. 55 the [[hhāsya|bhāṣya]] quotes several views, but prefers that of [[Jaigigavya.|Jaigīṣavya.]] On Y. [[8.|S.]] III. 18,17] the bhāṣya mentions the dialogue between [[Avatya|Āvaṭya]] and [[Jaigisa vya,|Jaigīṣavya,]] where the latter’s opinion is stated to be that from the point of view of [[hairulyn,|kaivalya,]] the happiness of contentment is also misery, though as compared to pleasures of sense contentment is supposed to be and may be called [[happiness|happiness.]]

In the Buddhacarita (XII) when Gautama (the future Buddha) approached a philosopher [[Arāda,|Arāḍa,]] the latter described to Gautama his idea of the path to [[moksa|mokṣa]] and mentioned [[Jaiglṣarya,|Jaigīṣavya,]] Janaka and [[Vrddha. Parāśara|Vṛddha-Parāśara]] as persons that had become liberated by following that [[path,2787|path.]]18

From these references it follows that [[Jaigisavya|Jaigīṣavya]] was a great teacher of Yoga long before the Christian era and probably [[bad|had]] composed a work on Yoga not now available.

There are numerous English translations of the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] (wholly or partly) and of the Bhāṣya and [[Vacaspati’s|Vācaspati’s]] Commentary, such as that by Dr. Rajendralal Mitra with text of [[Y, S.,|Y. S.,]] bhāṣya and com. [[Kājamārtaṇḍa|Rājamārtaṇḍa]] and an appendix (p. [[218 227)|218–227)]] noting 150 [[mgs.|mss.]] on Yoga (B. I. [[od. 1883 );|ed. 1883);]] Swami [[Viveka nanda’s|Vivekananda’s]] ‘Rājayoga’ (Vol. I. of collected works, ed. of [[1946 )|1946)]] pp. 200–304, which translates and explains all the [[sutras ;|sūtras;]] Dr. Ganganath Jha’s translation (Bombay 1907); translation by Rama Prasad (pub. by Panini office, [[Allababad|Allahabad]] 1910); [[transla tion|translation]] by Prof. J. H. Woods (in the Harvard Oriental Series, 1914) who read with the present author in Bombay for some months in 1909; Geraldine Coster in ‘Yoga and Western Psychology’ (London, 1934) translates and explains many of

[[P1393]]Patañjali’s sūtras (pp. 100–131); ‘Aphorisms of Yoga’ done into English by Shree Purohita Swami and Intro. by W. B. Yeats (Faber & Faber, London, 1937) with figures of [[siddhāsana,|Siddhāsana,]] [[Baddhapadmasana,|Baddhapadmāsana,]] [[Pascimottānāsada,|Paścimottānāsana,]] [[Bhujangāśana,|Bhujaṅgāsana,]] [[Viparit akaraṇl|Viparītakaraṇī]] and Matsyendrāsana; [[‘Bhāratiya|‘Bhāratīya]] [[Mānaga-śāstra’|Mānasa-śāstra’]] or [[‘Patañjala-yoga-darsana’|‘Pātañjala-yoga-darśana’]] (Sanskrit text, with a table of contents, errata, Introduction of 232 pages, and meaning and explanation pp. 787 in Marathi) in all 1051 pages, edited by Krishnaji [[Kashav|Keshav]] Kolhatkar and published by K. B. Dhavale, Bombay, 1951—a very exhaustive and useful work, though rather diffuse and marred by a few uncritical statements.

The number of works and papers on Yoga in general written by Indian and Western authors is very large. The present author has not read many of them. He may mention the following among those that he read or consulted; ‘Rājayoga’ (complete works of Vivekananda, ed. of 1946, Mayavati, [[vOL|vol.]] I. pp. 119–313); ‘Yoga technique in the Great Epic’ by W. Hopkins in JAOS vol. 22 for 1901 pp. 333–379; ‘Yoga as a philosophy and [[religion|religion’]] by Prof. [[8.|S.]] N. Das Gupta (London, 1924), and ‘Yoga [[philosophy|philosophy’]] (Uni. of Calcutta, 1930); Dr. J. W. [[Haūer’s|Haüer’s]] ‘Die [[Anfrange|Anfänge]] der Yogapraxis im Alten [[Indien ‘( Stuttgart, 1922 )|Indien’ (Stuttgart, 1922)]] and ‘Der Yoga Als Heilweg nach den Indischen quellen Dargestellt’, Stuttgart, 1932; this is a careful and systematic study of Yoga; this work (pp. 101–127) gives the transliterated text of the [[Yoga. Sūtra|Yoga-sūtra]] with translation in German not in serial order but by subjects (such as yogāṅgas, kriyāyoga, [[Isvaraprapidhāna);|Īśvarapraṇidhāna);]] Dr. Radhakrishnan’s ‘Indian Philosophy’ vol. II. pp. 336–373 (London, 1931); ‘The mysterious [[Kundalini’|Kuṇḍalinī’]] by Dr. J. G. Rele ([[Taraporovala|Taraporevala]] and sons, Bombay, 1927); ‘Yoga, the science of health’ by Felix Guyot (in French), translated by J. Carling, London, 1937 (3rd ed.), expounding the practical principles of Haṭha-yoga; ‘Yoga, a scientific19 evaluation’ by Dr. K. T. [[Bebanan,|Behanan,]] pub. by Macmillan & Co., New York, 1937 (the author studied at Kaivalya-dhāma for a year); ‘Tibetan Yoga and secret [[dootrine’|doctrine’]] by W. Y. Evans-Wentz (Oxford Uni. Press, 1935) and ‘Tibetan Book of the Dead’ by the same author (Oxford, 1927); ‘A search in secret India’ by Paul Brunton (London, 1947); [[Pąul|Paul]] Tuxen’s ’the religions of [[India|India’]] (Copenhagen, 1949);

[[P1394]]‘Tibetan Yoga’ by Bernard Bromage (2nd ed. of 1939) pp. 108–111; [[.Yoga|‘Yoga]] the method of re-integration’ by Allain Danielou (London, 1949); the author [[atates|states]] that the exposition is based on yoga works and also on the teachings of living exponents; it is a useful book with six illustrations and 352 Sanskrit texts; [[* The|‘The]] Tibetan Book of the Great Liberation’ ed. by W. G. Evans-Wentz (Oxford Uni. Press, 1954); pp. XXIX–LXIV contain C. G. Jung’s Psychological commentary and pp. 63–69 on astrology; ‘Source-book of Indian Philosophy’ by Dr. Radhakrishnan and C. A. Moore; gives translation of the whole yogasūtra with extracts from the [[bhāsya;|bhāṣya;]] [[Mercea|Mircea]] Eliade’s ‘Yoga, immortality and freedom’ translated from French into English by Willard R. Trask (London, 1958); ‘Hathayoga, [[|’]]an advanced method of physical education and concentration’ by Prof. [[8. 8.|S. S.]] Goswami (L. N. Fowler, London, 1959); this is a very valuable book; it deals scientifically with yoga in all its aspects, but particularly with āsanas of which 108 photographs are given; [[‘Con centration’|‘Concentration’]] by Mouni Sadhu (London, 1959); ‘The Lotus and the Robot’ by A. Koestler (London, 1960).

Many editions of the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] of Patañjali with the bhāṣya of Vyāsa and the commentary (called [[Tattvavaisāradi)|Tattvavaiśāradī)]] of Vācaspati have been printed in India. I shall mention only two or three editions of the text of the Sūtra and the two commentaries viz. the edition by the late Pandit Rajaram Shastri Bodas, printed in beautiful type at the [[Nirnayasagara|Nirṇayasāgara]] Press in 1892 and the other, the [[Anandaśrama|Ānandāśrama]] edition of 1932 which contains the sūtra, bhāṣya, [[Vacaspati’s|Vācaspati’s]] commentary and also the commentary called Rājamārtaṇḍa of king Bhoja. In the Kashi Sanskrit series the Yogasūtra was printed (in 1930) with six commentaries viz. the Rājamārtaṇḍa of Bhojarāja, the [[Pradipikā|Pradīpikā]] of Bhāvā [[Ganesa,|Gaṇeśa,]] [[vitti|vṛtti]] by [[Nāgoji-bhatta,|Nāgoji-bhaṭṭa,]] the [[Maṛi. prabbā|Maṇiprabhā]] by Rāmānandayati, the Candrikā by Anantadeva and the Yoga-sudhākara by Sadāśivendra Sarasvatī.20

The Yogasūtra, as compared with the sūtras of some other darśanas, is a brief one. It is divided into four [[pādus, vix.|pādas, viz.]]

[[P1395]][[Samadhi|Samādhi]] (concentration), [[Sadhana|Sādhana]] (means of attainment), Vibhūti (super-normal powers) and Kaivalya (aloofness, liberation). There are only 195 sūtras in all the pādas together (51+55+55+34).

Dr. Radhakrishnan in ‘Indian Philosophy’ ([[vol|vol.]] II. of the ed. of 1931 pp. 341–42) holds that the author of the Yogasūtra is not later than [[300 4. D.|300 A. D.]] Prof. [[8. N.|S. N.]] Das Gupta in ‘History of Indian Philosophy’ (vol. I. pp. 226–238) is in favour of the identity of the two Patañjalis and therefore holds that the author of the Yogasūtra flourished in the [[2nd century B.O.|2nd century B. C.]]21 The Sanskrit paper of Pandit R. [[Ramamurtiśarmā|Ramamūrtiśarmā]] in J. of [[Venkatesvara|Veṅkaṭeśvara]] Institute of Tirupati (vol. II. [[pp. 289-294|pp. 289–294]]) and Mr. Govind Das’s brief note in I. A. vol. 44 (for 1915 p. 24) may also be read in this connection. Jacobi and (following him) Keith hold that the Yogasūtra (I. 40), meaning at the most ’the mastery of the Yogin extends from the [[minutost|minutest]] particle to the greatest magnitude imaginable’,22 refers to the atomic theory of the world. This is a good sample of the way in which even great Western writers [[road|read]] in simple words later theories and try to give late dates to early works. The Upaniṣads speak of the self as more minute [[then|than]] what is [[anu|aṇu]] and as greater than the great and the Mahābhārata also uses the same [[phraseology, There|phraseology. There]] is no convincing reason to suppose that the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] refers only to a theory of atoms propounded in the [[Vaibeṣika|Vaiśeṣika]] system and is not paraphrasing the words of the Upaniṣad and the Mahābhārata.

We have also to consider the early tradition embodied in an introductory verse in Bhojadeva’s commentary (not later than [[1055 A, D.)|1055 A. D.)]] and mentioned by [[Cakrapāpi|Cakrapāṇi]] (commentator

[[P1396]]of the [[Carakasanhita 3291|Carakasaṁhitā]]23 (about 1060 A. D.) that [[Patakjall|Patañjali]] ([[doomed|deemed]] to be an [[avatūra|avatāra]] of [[Saṣa )|Śeṣa)]] composed works on Grammar, Yoga and Medicine.

The present author cannot deal at length in this volume with the two questions of identity and of the dates of the two works, as [[be|he]] is mainly concerned with the influence of Yoga on Dharmaśāstra works. He thinks that hardly any cogent [[argi. monts|arguments]] have been advanced to positively disprove the identity of the authors of the [[Mahabhāsya|Mahābhāṣya]] and of the [[Yogasutra.|Yogasūtra.]] Whether the revision of Caraka’s work was done by the same Patañjali is very doubtful. In the [[Sāntiparva|Śāntiparva]] the founder of Cikitsita (medicine) is said to have been [[Kṭspātreya|Kṛṣṇātreya]] and not Caraka nor Patañjali. The [[Caraka-sambitā|Caraka-saṁhitā]] uses the expression [[‘iti ha smāba bhagavān.Atreya)’|‘iti ha smāha bhagavān Ātreyaḥ’]] in the beginning of its chapters. Caraka (in I. 1. 23 ff) states that the sage [[Bharad āja|Bharadvāja]] learnt Āyurveda from [[Indra His|Indra. His]] pupil was [[Punarvagu|Punarvasu]] Ātreya, who had six disciples viz. Agniveśa, [[Bheda,|Bheḍa,]] [[Jatākarpa,|Jatūkarṇa,]] [[Parasara,|Parāśara,]] [[Harita|Hārīta]] and [[Kṣarapāni.|Kṣārapāṇi.]] First, [[Agnivesa|Agniveśa]] composed a [[treatine|treatise]] on Āyurveda and read it to [[Xtrega|Ātreya]] and then [[Bheda|Bheḍa]] and others did so. In the chapter called [[‘Tisraisapiya’|‘Tisraiṣaṇīya’]] in [[Caraka-Bardhita|Caraka-saṁhitā]] (I. 11. 75) [[Krspātreya’s|Kṛṣṇātreya’s]] view is specially mentioned. Therefore, it appears that [[Kroṇātreya|Kṛṣṇātreya]]24 is different from Ātreya who is [[rovergntly|reverently]] mentioned at the beginning of Caraka’s chapters. Even the Buddhacarita of Aśvaghoṣa mentions Ātreya as the first propounder of medical [[scienco.|science.]]

It is possible to argue that the tradition that Patañjali dealt with Yoga and Grammar is older than the [[Vakyapadiya|Vākyapadīya]] of [[Bhartphari.|Bhartṛhari.]] That work states in its first [[section 22%|section]]25 ([[Brahmakānda)|Brahmakāṇḍa)]] that the taints that affect the body, speech and

[[P1397]]intellect are purified ([[rospectively)|respectively)]] by the sciences of medicine, grammar and metaphysics. Then in the eulogy of the [[Maha bhāṣya|Mahābhāṣya]] it remarks [[‘alabdhagadhe|‘alabhdagādhe]] [[gāmbhīryād-uttana|gāmbhīryād-uttāna]] iva [[sausthavāt’,|sauṣṭhavāt’,]] on which the commentator explains that in the verse from the [[Brahmakāṇda|Brahmakāṇḍa]] the author of the Mahābhāṣya is praised and in the other verse there is praise of the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] itself. From this it appears to follow that according to the commentator the [[Vākyapadiya|Vākyapadīya]] attributes the three śāstras on medicine, grammar and metaphysics ([[i, e.|i. e.]] Yoga) to Patañjali himself.

Even if it be held that the authors of the Yogasūtra and of the Mahābhāṣya are different persons, there are hardly any reasons for definitely assigning the author of the Yogasūtra to a date later than the 2nd or 3rd century A. D. The date of the [[Yogabhāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] of Vyāsa is of considerable importance in arriving at the probable date of the Yogasūtra. But the question of the date of the Yogabhāṣya is also debatable. The [[Vyasa|Vyāsa]] who composed the [[Yogabbāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] would have to be held as different from Vedavyāsa, the reputed author of the Mahābhārata.

The author of the Yogasūtra is, according to the tradition embodied in such comparatively early commentaries as that of [[Vācaspatimisra,|Vācaspatimiśra,]] said to be [[Patanjali.|Patañjali.]] Important questions arise as to his age and his identity with Patañjali, the author of the famous [[Mahabhāsya|Mahābhāṣya]] on [[Pāṇini’a|Pāṇini’s]] grammar and the Vārtikas thereon. The grammarian Patañjali is generally held to have flourished about 150 [[B. O.|B. C.]] Therefore, the question of identity becomes important for the date of the [[Yogasutra.|Yogasūtra.]] Some scholars like Prof. B. Liebich, Dr. [[Hauer|Haüer]] (p. 98 of the work of 1932) and Prof. Das Gupta favour the identity of the two, but several others such as Jacobi, Keith, [[Wooda,|Woods,]] Renou are against it. Prof. [[Ronou|Renou]] (in [[I. E. Q. Vol.|I. H. Q. vol.]] XVI. pp. 586–591) examines the question from the grammatical point of [[vior,|view,]] points out that certain words like Pratyāhāra, Upasarga, Pratyaya are used in the Yogasūtra in senses entirely different from the senses of these words in the [[Mahabhāsya.|Mahābhāṣya.]] But as the subjects of the two works are entirely different, the same words may have acquired different senses. Similarly, Prof. Renou relies on breaches of grammatical rules (in the Yogasūtra I. 34), while he says that Patañjali in the [[Mahabhāṣya|Mahābhāṣya]] is very strict in following [[Pāpini’s|Pāṇini’s]] rules though [[Parini|Pāṇini]] himself is rarely not [[striot|strict]] in observing his own rules as in [[’tat-prayojako hotusca!|‘tat-prayojako hetuśca’]] (I. 4. 55) which is [[Against|against]] his own sūtra [[’trjakābhyām|‘tṛjakābhyāṁ]] kartari’ ([[IL.|II.]] 2. 15); but

[[P1398]][[Patanjali|Patañjali]] also employs such an expressions as ‘aviravikanyāya’26 (which should be [[avyavikanyayena|avyavikanyāyena]]) for which Patañjali [[ig oriticized|is criticized]] in the long [[Pūryapakṣa|Pūrvapakṣa]] on [[Vyakarana|Vyākaraṇa]] contained in the Tantravārtika and which is not explained away in the reply to the [[Puryapakṣa.|Pūrvapakṣa.]] It cannot be supposed that the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] for the first time settled the technical terms of [[Yoga Yoga|Yoga. Yoga]] terms had been evolving from [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] times and [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] only used them in the sense which they had acquired in the course of centuries. Prof. [[Ronou|Renou]] arrives at the conclusion that the Yogasūtra is later by several [[conturies|centuries]] than the [[Mahabhāsys,|Mahābhāṣya.]] Jacobi in his paper on ’the dates of the philosophical sūtras of the [[Brahmaṇas’|Brāhmaṇas’]] (in JAOS, vol. 31 pp. 1–29) holds (p. 29) that [[be|the]] Yogasūtra must be later [[that|than]] the 5th century A. D. and, following Garbe, thinks that it is not improbable that the [[Vyasabhasya|Vyāsabhāṣya]] was composed in the 7th century [[AD.|A. D.]] The views of Jacobi are strongly criticized by Jwala Prasad in J. R. A. [[8.|S.]] for 1930 pp. 365–375. The present author disagrees with Jacobi and Prof. [[Ronou.|Renou.]]

The date of the [[Yogabbāṣya|Yogabhāṣya]] will have considerable bearing on the date of the Yogasūtra. The [[Yogabhāsya|Yogabhāṣya]] presupposes much literary activity on Yoga. It [[mentiona|mentions]] by name on Y. [[8.|S.]] II. 55 and on III. 18 [[Jaiglṣavya|Jaigīṣavya]] who is a prominent figure in the Mahābhārata as shown above (pp. 1367 and 1374). Vide also the story of Asita [[Dovala|Devala]] with whom [[Jaiglṣavya atayed|Jaigīṣavya stayed]] for many years as a [[bbiksu|bhikṣu]] and adept in Yoga ([[Salyaparva,|Śalyaparva,]] chap. 50). It has to be remembered that several interpretations of the same [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] are noted in the bhāṣya ([[ag|as]] on II. 55). The Yogabhāṣya quotes several [[Kūrikūs|Kārikās]] and [[versos|verses]] dealing with the matters treated of in the Y. [[8.|S.]] as on Y. S. I. 28, 48, II. 5, 28 (on nine causes that lead to [[Vivekakbyati|Vivekākhyāti)]], II. 32, III. 6, [[JII, 15 (OD|III. 15 (on]] seven Cittadharmas of the [[apariltsta|apari-dṛṣṭa]] type). Besides these, several quotations in [[proso|prose]] are cited in the bhāṣya, many of which are attributed to [[Pancaśikha|Pañcaśikha]] by Vācaspati (vide pp. 1373–74 above), and some (as on II. 22, 30, 52) to āgamins (those who know the tradition or [[Voda).|Veda).]] Therefore, it follows that some centuries intervene between the Yogasūtra and its [[bhāgya.|bhāṣya.]]

The [[Bhagya|Bhāṣya]] on Y. [[8.|S.]] II. 42 quotes a verse with the words [[’ tatha|‘tathā]] coktam’ (it has also been said), which is identical with

[[P1399]]a verse from the Śāntiparva.27 It is most unlikely that an author will rely on his own work for supporting a proposition propounded in another work of his own. Besides, the Yogabhāṣya quotes on Y. S. I. 28 a verse, which is Viṣṇupurāṇa28 VI. 6. 2. The Viṣṇupurāṇa is one of the earliest among extant Purāṇas (vide above pp. 907–909) and cannot be assigned to a date later than the 3rd century A. D. Therefore, the Yogabhāṣya which quotes the Mahābhārata and the Viṣṇupurāṇa may be assigned to about 4th century A. D. As argued above, the Y. S. would therefore have to be assigned to a date not later than the 2nd or 3rd century A. D. Though the present writer holds that the Yoga that is said to be refuted in V. S. II. 1. 3 is not that of the Yogasūtra but the Yoga found in the Śāntiparva, yet he is not prepared to place the present Yogasūtra earlier than the 2nd century B. C. Mr. K. K. Kolhatkar in his very exhaustive and learned Introduction (in Marathi) asserts (on p. 126) that the Yogasūtra is later by 3000 years than the Brahmasūtra and that the Bhagavadgītā was composed about 5000 years before the present day (p. 224). But he adduces no evidence for such an ancient date for that work. He probably relies on the traditions that Vyāsa, the reputed author of the Mahābhārata (including the Gītā), flourished towards the end of the Dvāpara age and that Kaliyuga started in 3179 B. C. Modern critical scholarship does not accept these traditional datings.

Not only do some of the Upaniṣads contain references to Yoga technique and practices, but the Mahābhārata also dilates upon matters that pertain to the domain of Yoga. A few examples may be referred to here (the cr. ed. of the Mahābhārata is relied upon). In the Śāntiparva, chap. 232 (241 of Ch. ed.)

[[P1400]]it is [[stated 239|stated]]29 that kāma (desires), krodha (anger), lobha (covetousness), bhaya (fear) and svapna (sleep) are the five [[dosas ( disturbances ),|doṣas (disturbances),]] in the path of Yoga and then it [[proscribos|prescribes]] means for mastering them. An important and striking assertion in that chapter is that even a person of the lowest [[rurṇa ( class )|varṇa (class)]] or [[&|a]] woman who is intent on dharma may reach the highest goal by following this path30 (of Yoga). The same chapter [[laya|lays]] down (in verse 25) the proper places for the residence of a yogin and for the practice of Yoga, viz. untenanted mountains and caves, temples, vacant houses, in order that ekāgratā ([[one pointedness|one-pointedness]] of mind) may be achieved and that the yogin should treat in the same way a person who commends him or who speaks ill of him and should not contemplate the befalling of good or evil on any one. Chapter 289 of the [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] refers to [[Dhārapā ( verge 37 )|Dhāraṇā (verse 37)]] and states that a yogin that [[hag|has]] attained31 spiritual power may transfer himself to thousands of other bodies and move about in those bodies on the earth and that this path (of Yoga) is regarded as difficult of access even to wise brāhmaṇas and no one can tread it with ease and that [[ona|one]] can stand easily on the sharp edges of razors, but to abide in [[Dhāraña|Dhāraṇā]] of Yoga is difficult for those whose souls are not purified. Śāntiparva 304. 1 states that there is no knowledge equal to [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] and no spiritual power equal to Yoga; it further says that Yoga is eightfold ([[vorge 7 )|verse 7)]] and in verse 9 mentions

[[P1401]][[Dharana|Dhāraṇā]] and Prāṇāyāma. The [[Asvamedhikaparva|Āśvamedhikaparva]] 19. 17 (Ch. ed.) appears to refer to pratyāhāra.32

There is also striking similarity between the [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] and the Yogasūtra.33 For example, the definition of Yoga in the Yogasūtra as the ‘mastery over (or elimination of) the functions (or fluctuations) of the mind’ has a close parallel in the [[Gitā.|Gītā.]] The [[Gitā|Gītā]] insists on the yogin being [[‘aparigraba’|‘aparigraha’]] (VI. 10); [[‘aparigpha’|‘aparigraha’]] is one of the five yamas (Y. S. [[II, 30 ).|II. 30).]] Similarly, the seat and posture (āsana) in which a yogin is to practise must be stable (this refers to the seat) and comfortable (says [[Yogasūtra );|Yogasūtra);]] the [[Gitā|Gītā]] says the same thing in greater detail. In [[VIII, 12|VIII. 12]] the [[Gitā|Gītā]] speaks of [[Yogadharapā.|Yogadhāraṇā.]] The [[Gitā|Gītā]] says (in [[VI, 35 )|VI. 35)]] that the mind is certainly restless and difficult to control, but it can be controlled by practice and passionlessness and the Y. S. [[(I. 12 )|(I. 12)]] speaks of the same two means. The [[Gita|Gītā]] (in V. [[4-5)|4–5)]] emphasizes that it is the ignorant that regard [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] and Yoga as different, that he who applies himself or stands committed to one of these secures the fruit held out by both and that he who [[bees|sees]] both ways as identical [[soos|sees]] truly. [[Hore|Here]] [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] means (sannyāsa) ‘renunciation’ and Yoga means [[’ Karma yoga’,|‘Karma-yoga’.]]

The Yogasūtra of Patañjali nowhere expressly sets out its scheme of the evolution of the world. But it contains enough material to enable us to hold that it presupposes and accepts some of the [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] doctrines such as the theory of [[Pradhana,|Pradhāna,]] the three guṇas and their characteristics, the real nature of the individual self and kaivalya (the state of the soul in final liberation). This may be established by a few references to [[Y.S.|Y. S.]] [[Y, S.|Y. S.]] III. 48 describes the results that ensue to the yogin from subjugation or mastery of the organs (indriyas), one of which is

[[P1402]]‘pradhānajaya’34 (subjugation of [[Pradhana. the|Pradhāna, the]] primary cause of the world according to the [[Bankhya).|Sāṅkhya).]] The [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] nowhere describes what [[Pradhana|Pradhāna]] is and what its evolutes are. Therefore, it follows that it takes over from the Sāṅkhya all that is [[geid|said]] about [[Pradhana.|Pradhāna.]] About the individual soul the [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] states ’the seer [[(Purusa)|(Puruṣa)]] [[exiats zz03|exists]]35 as merely pure capacity of awareness (or as the mere power of seeing) and although pure ([[changeles|changeless]] or undefiled or free from any taints) appears as if he [[soos|sees]] all [[tiporiences that really pertain to Buddhi).’|experiences (that really pertain to Buddhi)’.]] The characteristics of the three guṇas (sattva, rajas and tamas) are clearly and [[succintly 1305|succinctly]]36 stated in [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] as in [[8.|S.]] Kārikā 13, when it [[gaye *tbe|says ’the]] seen (phenomenal universe) is characterized by [[light (sattva ),|light (sattva),]] [[activity (rajas )|activity (rajas)]] and inertia or dullness (tamas), it is the essence of the elements and organs and it exists for the purpose of giving experience and liberation (to the soul)’. The guṇas are frequently referred to in [[Y. 8. 1. 16,|Y. S. I. 16,]] IV. 13, 32, 34 and [[satraguna|sattvaguṇa]] in Y. S. [[II, 41,|II. 41,]] III. 35, 49 and 55. The [[Y. 8.|Y. S.]] postulates three [[pramūnas (in 1.7),|pramāṇas (in I. 7),]] but does not define them; the [[8.|S.]] [[Kāriki (4-6)|Kārikā (4–6)]] mentions the same three [[pramāgas|pramāṇas]] and briefly defines them. They both agree about the plurality of souls. It may further be noted that [[Vyasabhāsya|Vyāsabhāṣya]] on Y. S. is full of Sāṅkhya doctrines and quotes, according to Vācaspati, [[Pancasikha|Pañcaśikha]] twelve times and [[Ṣasṭitantra|Ṣaṣṭitantra]] once (vide above p. 1374 and [[. 2250 ).|n. 2250).]]

In spite of the fact that the Yogasūtra accepts some of the fundamental doctrines of the [[Bankhya,|Sāṅkhya,]] there are certain points of difference between the two. The standard Sāṅkhya finds [[zo placa|no place]] for God ([[Isvara )|Īśvara)]] i. e. it is frankly atheistic ([[ if| (if]] theism means belief in God who is the creator of the universe and regulator of rewards and punishments), while Yoga finds a place for God in [[Y. 8. 1. 23-29,|Y. S. I. 23–29,]] though not a basic one, but only secondary, probably as a concession to popular feelings and belief. The

[[P1403]][[Y. 8,|Y. S.]] nowhere expressly says that [[Isvara|Īśvara]] is the creator of the world and all that it says about Him is that in him omniscience is at its highest, that He was the teacher of primal sages and that by repetition of the [[mystio|mystic]] syllable ‘om’ and reflection on it the yogin attains correct knowledge of the real nature of the self. In the next place, though the summum bonum in both systems is called kaivalya ([[8.|S.]] Kārikā 64, 68 and [[Y. 8. III, 50, 55, IV, 34 ),|Y. S. III. 50, 55, IV. 34),]] yet the Sāṅkhya does not [[get|set]] out any elaborate discipline for attaining it except correct knowledge, that is, it is metaphysical and rational, while the [[Y, S.|Y. S.]] gives an elaborate treatment on mind discipline, insists on effort rather than [[inere|mere]] jñāna, attaches great importance to prāṇāyāma and meditation (dhyāna) and is psychological.

The Sāṅkhya postulated that an intellectual understanding of the nature of [[purusa|puruṣa]] and of [[praksti|prakṛti]] (or [[gunas )|guṇas)]] and of the difference between the two was sufficient for the emancipation of the individual self from the liability to rebirth, while yoga, on the other hand, was not content with this philosophic easy-going mental frame and emphasized systematic training of the will and emotions. In both Sāṅkhya and Yoga each individual soul is eternal and his destiny is to become free from the influence of Prakṛti and its evolutes and to remain for ever the same ([[vig.|viz.]] pure intelligence). Herein they differ from the Advaita Vedānta, [[According|according]] to which the final destiny of the self is to become absorbed in and one with brahma.

There is another matter for consideration. In the [[Yājña valkya-smrti, Yajnavalkya|Yājñavalkya-smṛti, Yājñavalkya]] is put forward as saying that the [[Boul|soul]] shining like a lamp in the heart should be realized, that on such realization the soul is not born again and as adding that for the purpose of attaining to Yoga one should understand the [[Aranyaka|Āraṇyaka]]37 which ‘I received from the Sun and also the

[[P1404]]Yogaśāstra propounded by me.’ In the [[Kūrmapurana it 18 Baid.|Kūrmapurāṇa it is said]] that [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] composed Yogaśāstra at the order of [[Hara’and|Hara and]] the [[Vispupurāpa (IV. 4 107)|Viṣṇupurāṇa (IV. 4. 107)]] states that [[Hirapyanābha|Hiraṇyanābha]] attained the knowledge of Yoga from the great [[Yoglāvara Yajnavalkya|Yogīśvara Yājñavalkya]] who was a pupil of Jaimini. In the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Up. (II. 4) [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] tells his wife [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] (who hankered after immortality and cared [[daught|naught]] for worldly goods) that he would expound to her the path to immortality and the very first sentence calls upon her to cultivate [[’nididhyāga’ (i. e, dhyana)|‘nididhyāsa’ (i. e. dhyāna)]] and the first part of his discourse [[onds|ends]] with the memorable words [[(ātmā vā are drastavyaḥ śrotavyo mantavyo nididhy. sitavyah, Bṛ, Up. II, 4.5).|(‘ātmā vā are draṣṭavyaḥ śrotavyo mantavyo nididhyāsitavyaḥ’, Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 5).]] What work is meant by the [[Yoga Śāstra|Yogaśāstra]] composed by Yāj. is a debatable point. There are three works (other than the [[Yāj. smrti)|Yāj. smṛti)]] connected with the name of Yājñavalkya viz. … [[Vļddha-Yājñavalkya, Yoga-Yajñavalkya and Bṭbad-yogi-yājñavalkya|Vṛddha-Yājñavalkya, Yoga-Yājñavalkya and Bṛhad-yogi-yājñavalkya]] (vide H. of Dh. [[Vol I. p. 188-190).|vol. I. pp. 188–190).]] The last has [[beon|been]] published by [[Kaivalyadbama,|Kaivalyadhama,]] Lonavla (1951), the [[Yoga-Yaj.|Yoga-Yāj.]] was published in T. S. S. (1938) based on a single ms., then in the JBBRAS ([[vol. 28 and 29 )|vol. 28 and 29)]] by Shri P. C. Diwanji and now available in book form (1954). This last is in the form of a dialogue between the great yogin [[Yājöavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] and [[Gārgi (who is identified with Maitreyi|Gārgī (who is identified with Maitreyī]] by Shri Diwanji on [[pp. 27-28 of his ed. of 1954 )|pp. 27–28 of his ed. of 1954)]] and sages and learned brāhmaṇas. Yājñavalkya narrates what he learnt from Brahmā. On Yāj. III. 110 the [[Dipakalikā of Sulapāṇi|Dīpakalikā of Śūlapāṇi]] remarks that the [[Yogasāstra referred to is called. Yogiyājñavalkya’.|Yogaśāstra referred to is called ‘Yogiyājñavalkya’.]] But this again leads to uncertainty as both the works viz. that published by [[Kaivalya dhama|Kaivalyadhama]] and the one by Mr. Diwanji are [[80|so]] referred to as Yogi (or Yoga) [[Yajñavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] in several digests. Mr. [[Diwanjia306|Diwanji]]38 tries hard to establish that the work published by [[hin|him]] ‘has a better claim to be adjudged the specific work on Yoga referred to in [[Yaj. III. 113.’|Yāj. III. 113.’]] I think that the claim is not at all justified. The work edited by Mr. Diwanji no doubt contains a good deal of Yoga knowledge; while the work published by Kaivalyadhama also contains [[&|a]] good deal of Yoga material, though not as much as the work edited by Mr. Diwanji [[containg.|contains.]] It would be irrelevant to discuss here all the points made by Mr. Diwanji. The [[procent|present]] author is convinced that the work sponsored by Mr. Diwanji is not the work referred to by [[Yājóavalkya (in his smrti|Yājñavalkya (in his smṛti]] III. 110). It is a compilation of a later period. Some striking

[[P1405]]matters alone may be pointed out to show that the [[Yoga Yājñavalkya oould|Yoga-Yājñavalkya could]] not have been the work of the author of the [[Bpbadāraṇyaka|Bṛhadāraṇyaka]] and the Yogaśāstra (as stated in Yāj. III. [[110 )|110)]] and of [[Yaj, smrti.|Yāj. smṛti.]] In the Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 1 and [[IV.5.1|IV. 5. 1]] ([[Yājña valkyasya|Yājñavalkyasya]] dve bhārye [[babhūvatur-Maitreyi ca Katyayani ca )|babhūvatur-Maitreyī ca Kātyāyanī ca)]] it is expressly stated that [[Yajāavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] had two wives, [[(philosophy minded) Maitreyi and Kātýāyan!|(philosophy-minded) Maitreyī and Kātyāyanī]] (worldly-minded). [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] desired such knowledge as would lead to immortality and she asks questions, in all of which she addresses [[Yājsavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] as [[’ bhagavān’|‘bhagavān’]] (in Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 3 and 13 and IV. 5. 4, 14) and never as mere Yājñavalkya. On the other hand, [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] in the [[Bṛ, Up,|Bṛ. Up.]] is called [[Vacaknavi (in III. 6. 1, 1II. 8.1|Vācaknavī (in III. 6. 1, III. 8. 1]] and [[12 ),|12),]] is not [[Yajnavalkya’s|Yājñavalkya’s]] wife but a pert and rationalistic woman inquirer like [[Afvala, Artabhāga,|Aśvala, Ārtabhāga,]] Bhujyu Lāhyāyani, Uṣasta Cākrāyaṇa, Kahola (all present in the court of Janaka), who including [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] questioned the claim of [[Yājöavalkya|Yājñavalkya]] to be regarded as [[’ brahmiṣtha’.|‘brahmiṣṭha’.]] In Bṛ. Up. [[III, 6.1|III. 6. 1]] when [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] pursues her ratiocination too far (in matters depending upon āgama and being beyond [[reasoning )|reasoning)]] Yājñavalkya [[reprimande|reprimands]] her and says that she would die ([[lit:|lit.]] her head would fall away) if she pursued the subject in [[& pure logistic|a pure logistic]] way. All [[those|these]] inquirers address Yājñavalkya as mere Yājñavalkya without any [[honorifio|honorific]] epithet like ‘bhagavān’ and [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] does the same (in Bṛ. Up. [[III. 6, 1,|III. 6. 1,]] III. 8. 2–6). [[Aoc, to Yāj. Smrti|Acc. to Yāj. Smṛti]] III. 110 the [[Br. Up., a Yoga. śāstra and the smrti|Bṛ. Up., a Yogaśāstra and the smṛti]] are the works of one man (whose two wives were [[Maitreyl and Katyayani)|Maitreyī and Kātyāyanī)]] and who had a philosophical skirmish with [[Gārgl Vācaknavi ( according to the Br. Up.).|Gārgī Vācaknavī (according to the Bṛ. Up.).]] The present Yogayājñavalkya (ed. by Mr. Diwanji) makes [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] wife of Yājñavalkya39 ([[vadhūḥ).|(vadhūḥ).]] One would like to know whether [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] had three wives, as [[Bș.|Bṛ.]] Up. says he had only two. Mr. Diwanji ([[pp. 27-28|pp. 27–28]] of the [[ed,|ed.]] of 1954) tries to make light of this [[discrepanoy|discrepancy]] and regards [[Maitreyi|Maitreyī]] as another name of [[Gārgi.|Gārgī.]] We are here [[con cerned|concerned]] not with pure Yoga [[dootrines|doctrines]] but with the question

[[P1406]]whether a work attributed to [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] which makes [[Gārgi|Gārgī]] the wife of the ancient [[Yājāavalkya (|Yājñavalkya (]]when the Upaniṣad makes her only a more or [[loss|less]] insolent woman) can be regarded as the work of the same Yāj. who propounded brahmavidyā in the [[Br. Up.|Bṛ. Up.]] and is supposed to have composed the Yāj. [[smrti.|smṛti.]] This [[ono circums bance|one circumstance]] alone is [[onough|enough]] to brand the [[Yoga-Yajñavalkya (|Yoga-Yājñavalkya (]]of Mr. Diwanji) as a fabrication sought to be passed off as an ancient work. If identity was really meant the [[verso|verse]] could easily [[bave|have]] been read as ‘Maitreyyākhyā mahābhāgā’ without any metrical fault. Therefore, it is not possible to hold that Yogayājñavalkya is the Yogaśāstra composed by Yājñavalkya before the [[Smrti|Smṛti]] going under his name was composed. A few other decisive grounds may also be urged. The work edited by Mr. Diwanji [[moptions|mentions]] Tantras [[( in V. 10)|(in V. 10)]] and [[Tantrikas (in VIII. 4 and 25 ).|Tāntrikas (in VIII. 4 and 25).]] The Yāj. [[smrti|smṛti]] nowhere mentions these two and is free from the peculiar [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] words or technique. Therefore, the present Yogayājñavalkya edited by Mr. Diwanji was composed long after the Yāj. [[smrti|smṛti]] and probably in the 8th century or after when [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] rites and works had become common. One more important matter may be adverted to. The Yāj. [[sınti|smṛti]] and the Yoga-Yājñavalkya [[( ed. by Mr. Diwanji )|(ed. by Mr. Diwanji)]] both enumerate ten yamas and ten niyamas. But the two diverge materially in the ten names as the note below will [[show, 2008|show.]]40 The number of yamas and niyamas differs in different works, but if the Yāj. [[smrti|smṛti]] and Yoga-Yājñavalkya were the works of the same author, the differences in the ten names would not have [[ocourred|occurred]] at all. Therefore, the author of the Yāj. [[empti|smṛti]] is quite different from the author of the Y. Y. There is no evidence for holding that the latter flourished before the [[Sth|8th]] or 9th century A. D.

A good deal has been said by Mr. Diwanji in his paper on [[’ * Bṛbad-yogi-yājñavalkya|‘Bṛhad-yogi-yājñavalkya]] and [[Yoga-Yajñavalkya’|Yoga-Yājñavalkya’]] in [[ABORI, VOL.|ABORI, vol.]] XXXIV (1953) [[pp. 1-29,|pp. 1–29,]] in his preface to [[Yoga-Yājāavalky&|Yoga-Yājñavalkya]] in [[JBBRAS, volumes XXXVILI|JBBRAS, volumes XXVIII]] and XXXIX [[pp. 103-106|pp. 103–106]] and by Swami Kuvalayanand in his reply in ABORI, vol. XXXVII for

[[P1407]]1957 [[pp. 279-289|pp. 279–289]] and in the paper [[’the real Yogayajāavalkya: smrti’|‘the real Yogayājñavalkya-smṛti’]] in the journal [[‘Yogamimārnsa’ VOL VII,|‘Yogamīmāṁsā’ vol. VII,]] No. 2 (and also published separately as a pamphlet in July 1958). It would not be relevant in this work to discuss the points in dispute between Mr. Diwanji and Swami Kuvalayanand. In the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on the [[Svetāśvataropaniṣad|Śvetāśvataropaniṣad]] attributed to [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]]41 (published by [[Anan, Press)|Ānan. Press)]] on p. 28 four [[Ferses|verses]] and a half are quoted from [[Yogi-Yajśavalkya,|Yogi-Yājñavalkya,]] none of which is found either in Bṛ. Y. Y. or Y. Y. Besides on [[Sv. Up. II. 9|Śv. Up. II. 9]] ([[pp. 42-44)|pp. 42–44)]] 29 verses on Yoga matters are quoted in the [[bhāsya,|bhāṣya,]] but the name of the author or work whence the quotations are taken is not mentioned. Not one of the 29 verses wholly occurs in Mr. [[Diwanji’s Y, Y.|Diwanji’s Y. Y.]] He is able to point out only five or six half verses as occurring in [[Y, Y.|Y. Y.]] out of the 29 [[versos,|verses,]] while a whole [[yerse|verse]] quoted in the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] [[(viz.‘prāṇāyāmair’ &c )|(viz. ‘prāṇāyāmair’ &c)]] occurs in Bṛ. Y. Y. (8. [[32 ). (It|32). It]] may be further pointed out that Aparārka and the [[Smrticandrika|Smṛticandrikā]] quote in all about 100 verses from Yogi (or Yoga-) [[Yājśavalky&|Yājñavalkya]] which are found in [[Bs. Y. Y.,|Bṛ. Y. Y.,]] but not in Y. Y. The Kṛtyakalpataru (on [[Mokṣakāṇda alone )|Mokṣakāṇḍa alone)]] quotes (on [[pp. 146, 149, 166, 171, 196-197)|pp. 146, 149, 166, 171, 196–197)]] about 70 verses from [[Yogi-yājāvalky&|Yogi-yājñavalkya]] which are found in chapters 2, 8, 9 and 11 of the [[Br. Y. Y,|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] Mr. Diwanji has not been able to show that verses of the Y. Y. [[(ed, by him )|(ed. by him)]] are cited anywhere in the above three digests. Mr. Bhabatosh [[Bhattacarya|Bhattacharya]] in his paper on [[Yogi-Yajāavalkya-gmṛti|Yogi-Yājñavalkya-smṛti]] and its utilization in the medieval digests of Bengal and Mithila (in [[J.G. J. R. I, vol XV for 1958 pp. 135–140 )|J. G. J. R. I. vol. XV for 1958 pp. 135–140)]] points out that king Ballālasena of Bengal ([[1158–1179 A, D.)|1158–1179 A. D.)]] in his Dānasāgara contains a fairly large number of quotations from the [[Bșhad-Yogi Yaj. (pub.|Bṛhad-Yogi Yāj. (pub.]] by Kaivalya-dhama). It appears that [[Viśvarūpa2210|Viśvarūpa]]42 (first half of 9th century A.D.) quotes a half verse from the [[Br. Y.Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] and remarks that the work is composed by the author of the Yāj.

[[P1408]][[smati himself Therefore,|smṛti himself. Therefore,]] the [[Br. Y.Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] is an early work and cannot be assigned to a [[dato|date]] later than the 7th century A.D.; while Y.Y. is a much later [[oompilation|compilation]] composed in the 8th or 9th century or even later. The present author is not, however, prepared to hold that [[Br. Y. Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] is the Yogaśāstra of Yāj. mentioned in Yāj. [[sifti III, 110,|smṛti III. 110,]] since the Yoga material contained in it is meagre as compared with the [[Smrti|Smṛti]] material. The present author [[ia|is]] glad to find that on [[pp. 7-8|pp. 7–8]] of the Sanskrit Preface to the edition of the [[Bșhad-Yogi-Yājñavalkya-smrti|Bṛhad-Yogi-Yājñavalkya-smṛti]] the editors express grave doubts about the identity of the author of the Yāj. smṛti with the author of the work edited by them.

The [[Yogavāsiṣtha|Yogavāsiṣṭha]] is an extensive work in 32000 [[ślokag (ope sloka|Ślokas (one śloka]] meaning 32 syllables) printed in two volumes with [[& gomventary by Anandabodha by the Nirn.|a commentary by Ānandabodha by the Nirṇ.]] Press. It is really an eclectic work [[embodying 21|embodying]]43 the tenets of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] on anāsakti, the tenets of the Trika system of Kashmir, of Advaita [[Vedanta|Vedānta]] &c. Its text appears to have been added to from time to time. Controversies have raged about its date and its contribution to Indian philosophy. The present writer holds on reading the contributions44 that the extant [[Yogavāsiṣtha|Yogavāsiṣṭha]] is a late work and was composed some time between the 11th and 13th century A. D.

It is now necessary to furnish a brief outline of the main teachings of Y. S. Though the work is comparatively small, [[it!|its]] rendering in English requires to be amplified in order that it may be understandable. Only the most important and [[instruc tive|instructive]] matters are set out here.

[[Yogi|Yoga]] is defined (2nd sūtra) as the elimination of or mastery over the functions (activities) or fluctuations of the mind. This is provisionally explained by [[Vyasa ( on 1. 1.)|Vyāsa (on I. 1.)]] as

[[P1409]][[Samadhi.|Samādhi.]] The different stages [[viz. bhūmis) of the mind ase|(viz. bhūmis) of the mind are]] five, being restless [[(ksipta), infatuated (mugdha or mudha), distracted (viksipta), one-pointed (ekagra) and controlled (niruddha.).|(kṣipta), infatuated (mugdha or mūḍha), distracted (vikṣipta), one-pointed (ekāgra) and controlled (niruddha).]]

In this connection [[(i.e. on the topic of bhumis) the present . author|(i. e. on the topic of bhūmis) the present author]] has to bring to the notice of readers a paper by Shri Kuvalayānanda contributed to the [[Yogamlmāmgā vol VI.|Yogamīmāṁsā vol. VI.]] No. 4 on ‘Traditional reading of Pātañjala-yogasūtra III. 11; is it accurate.’ The traditional reading accepted by the bhāṣya of Vyāsa and all other commentators is [[‘Sarvārthataikāgratayoh kṣayodayau cittasya samadhipariṇāmah’.|‘Sarvārthataikāgratayoḥ kṣayodayau cittasya samādhipariṇāmaḥ’.]] The [[Svami2314|Svami]]45 argues that the correct reading should be [[‘sarvarthatailarthayoh|‘sarvārthataikārthayoḥ]] &c.’ He states that the bhūmis should be six, the sixth being ’ekārtha’ and bases his arguments on the combined explanations of Yoga sūtra I. [[2-4, 18 and III. 9-12.|2–4, 18 and III. 9–12.]] All that the present writer can say is that his arguments deserve serious consideration. The fact that even Vyāsa, the [[bhāsyakāra|bhāṣyakāra]] of Yogasūtra, would have to be held not to have seen the difficulties in the traditional reading of Y. S. III. 11 would have to be given due weight before any final conclusion can be arrived at. That sūtra states the goal of Yoga viz. the soul that is seer abides in its own form then [[(i.e.|(i. e.]] when the functions of the mind have been mastered), while in ordinary life the soul appears to assume the forms of the [[fluctua tions|fluctuations]] of the mind. The [[Vșttis B15|Vṛttis]]46 are five, some of which are

[[P1410]][[affiliated|afflicted]] by hindrances called [[klesas|kleśas]] and the others are [[pot 80|not so]] hindered (those that are hindered are to be mastered or eliminated and the others are to be accepted). The five [[vpliis ,|vṛttis]] are pramūṇa [[(means of valid knowledge ), viparyaya (wrong Conception), rikalpa (fancy),|(means of valid knowledge), viparyaya (wrong conception), vikalpa (fancy),]] [[nidrū (sleep),|nidrā (sleep),]]47 [[smrti|smṛti]] (memory). [[Pramanas|Pramāṇas]] are three, [[pratyakṣa (perception), anumāna (inference), agama|pratyakṣa (perception), anumāna (inference), āgama]] (verbal testimony). The means for the suppression [[of: the vșttis are abhyūsu (practice) and rurūgya (passionlessness )|of the vṛttis are abhyāsa (practice) and vairāgya (passionlessness)]] (simultaneously carried on), the first being the effort to secure a calm flow of the mind free from [[vșttis,|vṛttis,]] continued for a long time uninterruptedly and earnestly and the latter (vairāgya) being the consciousness of mastery over (i. e. freedom from thirst for) seen objects (such as woman, food and drink, high [[position )|position)]] and objects promised by Revelation (such as heaven, disembodied existence [[&c.), 2317 Vairagya|&c.),]]48 ‘Vairāgya is of two kinds apara (described in Y. S. I. 15 just above) and para (highest) described in Y. S. I. 16 and [[bhāsya theroon.|bhāṣya thereon.]] In the highest [[cairāgya|vairāgya]] the yogin (who has reached discrimination between the self and the [[quṇas,|guṇas,]] sattva &c.) is free from thirst not only for objects of sense, but also free from the [[gunas,|guṇas,]] attains [[& stage of updisturbed|a stage of undisturbed]] consciousness only and leads the yogin to reflect [[.I|‘I]] have attained what was to be attained, the [[klesas (hindrancos|kleśas (hindrances]]

[[P1411]][[a vidya &o.)|avidyā &c.)]] that have to be destroyed have been destroyed, the close-knit succession of births and deaths has been [[out|cut]] off’. The Bhāṣya remarks ’the highest limit of consciousness [[(knowledge )|(knowledge)]] is Vairāgya and Kaivalya is inseparably connected with it.’ [[Vāoaspati|Vācaspati]] states that this last is called [[’ Dharmamegha-samadhi’|‘Dharmamegha-samādhi’]] (mentioned in Y. S. IV. 29). Sūtras 17 and 18 of first pāda deal respectively with [[samprajñāta samadhi|samprajñāta-samādhi]] (conscious concentration) or [[sālambabasamādhi|sālambanasamādhi]] and [[asamprajñāta-samādbi|asamprajñāta-samādhi]] (concentration in which there is no [[con sciousness|consciousness]] of objects, gross or subtle). The first is of four kinds (or of four stages) viz. savitarka (deliberation over or [[realiza tion|realization]] of a gross object like [[Sālagrāma|Śālagrāma]] or the image of a god with four arms and the like), savicāra [[( where|(where]] there is reflection over [[&|a]] subtle object such as the tan-mātrās), sānanda where the reflection is on the mind which is full of sattva (that is a [[samadhi of joy )|samādhi of joy)]] and sāsmitarūpa (i. e. where there is only a sense of personality in which the knower himself is the [[objeot|object]] of [[perception). 2318|perception).]]49 These four kinds lead on to the [[asamprajñātam|asamprajñāta-]]samādhi, which results when all [[vșttis|vṛttis]] have disappeared, when there is persistent exercise of this state and the mind consists of [[subliminal|sub-liminal]] impressions only. Sūtras 19-51 (to the end of the first pāda) deal with the different kinds of [[samadhi,|samādhi,]] the different ways of attainment, the position of [[Iśvara|Īśvara]] in the Yoga system, the nine obstacles (antarāya) that cause distraction of the mind of the person who [[practices|practises]] Yoga and the companions of these

[[P1412]]obstacles, the means of preventing obstacles &c. such as practice of concentration on a single entity, the cultivation of friendliness, compassion, joy (towards holy men), indifference (towards unholy men) or by prāṇāyāma.

The Pātañjalasūtra (in I. 19–23) divides yogins for asamprajñāta-samādhi into various categories (nine in all), which are passed over here. Patañjali says that asamprajñāta-samādhi in the case of yogins (who are not gods or not those whose bodies are resolved into primary matter) follows upon that practice which effects the cessation of all vṛttis and which comprises only subliminal consciousness and is preceded by faith, perfect repose or serenity, energy, mindfulness and discriminatory insight (into what is real) due to passionlessness. It would be noticed that so far Patañjali asks the aspirant for Yoga to rely on his own efforts, practice &c. He does not mention God or his grace anywhere so far. From this it follows that any man or woman and even one who is an agnostic may pursue yogic practices and attain to asamprajñāta-samādhi and liberation without devotion to God or without God’s grace. But Patañjali as a keen psychologist knew the help that faith affords. Therefore, he provides that, if you believe in God, He will help you in the practice of Yoga and from that point of view he assigns to Īśvara a role of a limited scope.

In Y. S. I. 23–28 Patañjali provides that Samādhi and liberation (the result of Samādhi) may also be secured by50

[[P1413]] devotion to [[Isvara|Īśvara]] ([[1śvara-prapidhāna|īśvara-praṇidhāna]]) who is a special [[purusa|puruṣa]], who is untouched by hindrances, by karma (good or evil) or by the results of [[aotions|actions]] or by [[bankerings|hankerings]], in whom the [[omnisci ence|omniscience]], that is only [[& gorm ( in others )|a germ (in others)]], is infinite; He, being not limited by time, is the teacher of even the ancient teachers. The mystic syllable (Om) is [[exprassive|expressive]] of (i. e. representative or symbol of) God and japa of that syllable and constant reflection upon the meaning of ‘om’ [[ |]](i. e. on [[Isvara|Īśvara]]) leads to [[one-pointed ness|one-pointedness]] ([[ekāgrata|ekāgratā]]) of mind. Devotion to [[Tývara|Īśvara]] secures for the practitioner attainment of right knowledge of the nature of the soul and the absence of hindrances (antarāya) that distract the mind [[(1. 29)|(I. 29)]]. These [[bindrances|hindrances]] are nine such as illness, [[langour|languor]], doubt &c. and they are also called Yogamala (taints or blemishes of Yoga), the enemies ([[prutipakṣa|pratipakṣa]]) of Yoga. These hindrances [[aro aocompanied|are accompanied]] by pain, mental distress, tremor of the limbs, irregular inhalation and exhalation (I. 31). These distractions and their accompaniments that are the [[foos|foes]] of samādhi may be prevented or mastered in various ways such as practice of [[con centration|concentration]] on one entity ([[Isvara|Īśvara]] or the like), by the practice of such attitudes of mind as friendliness, compassion, [[cheerful ness|cheerfulness]] and indifference respectively towards the happy or the distressed, the good and the evil [[(133)|(I. 33)]] or by the expulsion and retention of breath (bhāṣya uses the word ‘prāṇāyāma’). When the mind is concentrated then arise the four kinds of [[samprajñā. tanamādhi|samprajñātasamādhi]] (called savitarka &c. in I. 17). The knowledge that is gained in the last kind of samprajñātasamādhi is of a higher order than what is obtained from scripture or inference and the subliminal impression made in this samādhi is hostile to all other impressions and when this last impression also is [[suppr. essed|suppressed]] or removed, then results the seedless samādhi (i. e. [[asampr ajñātasamadhi|asamprajñātasamādhi]]). In this last the mind itself ceases ([[fron|from]] its task) and the yogin’s soul [[iabides|abides]] in itself, shines forth in its

[[P1414]] own light and is called pure, isolated and [[liberatod, 23:0|liberated.]]51 [[Isvara pranidhana dose not load|Īśvarapraṇidhāna does not lead]] to union with [[Isvara|Īśvara]], but it enables the soul that is still bound to become what [[Isvara|Īśvara]] always is. In the Yogasūtra [[thoro|there]] is very little about bhakti for God.

[[Pada|Pāda]] II. The first pāda ends on the note of samādhi and liberation i. [[e,|e.]] describes the Yoga for him who has succeeded in concentration. The 2nd pāda deals with the procedure for [[&|a]] person whose [[miod|mind]] is not used to concentration but is [[rostless|restless]], infatuated or distracted (vyutthita)52 and who is desirous of learning the technique. This pāda is the most important of all the four [[padas|pādas]] for modern students, either Indian or Western, and has exerted great influence on Dharmaśāstra works. The fundamental idea of Yoga is that the individual self is real, eternal and pure, but it is immersed in the objective world and, though eternal, pursues transient objects. [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] is a master psychologist. The goal being set ([[viz|viz.]] how to secure the [[isola tion|isolation]] of the soul from [[aridya|avidyā]] and the [[gunos|guṇas]] and its attainment of its own pure [[natu-re)|nature)]] Y. S. [[proscribes|prescribes]] a rigorous discipline for the attainment of the goal. [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] differs from many modern Western psychologists (like Freud)53 in two very fundamental matters. The first is that Patañjali puts all emphasis at [[bis comipand|his command]] on the freedom and liberation of the soul from [[bond. age|bondage]], prescribes restraint of the activities of the mind as the

m edi veg on 1. . I. 51 (24g fatto Fratar ia: HAITT:).|तस्मिन् (चित्ते) निवृत्ते पुरुषः स्वरूपमात्रप्रतिष्ठोऽतः शुद्धः केवलो मुक्त इत्युच्यते । भाष्य on यो. सू. I. 51 (तस्यापि निरोधे सर्वनिरोधान्निर्बीजः समाधिः) ।]]

[[P1415]] means and several preliminary steps of the training of ordinary emotions and will, while several modern psychologists condemn repression of the mind. In the 2nd place Patañjali is a firm believer in the doctrine of karma and rebirth and (in II. 12-15) holds that even good actions that give rise to a future life of pleasure and happiness are a misery to the enlightened, while modern psychologists speak of several innate [[instinots|instincts]] and are not at all agreed on what these are, they do not tackle the problem of karma and rebirth and their relation to what they call instincts. If there is no [[pre-existince|pre-existence]] of the soul as Christians and many others believe, how do the human instincts arise? There is hardly any convincing or satisfactory explanation of this.

The first sūtra (of pāda II) says that the activities or practices that are preliminaries to the attainment of Yoga are tapas54 (austerity), svādhyāya and devotion to Īśvara; these,

[[P1416]] when resorted to, serve for the cultivation of samādhi and for minimising the kleśas, which are avidyā (nescience which is the field that produces the other four), asmitā (the feeling of personality), rāga (attachment to pleasures), dveṣa (aversion, that dwells angrily on pain and its causes) and abhiniveśa (will to live or clinging to life). Tapas is explained by Vyāsabhāṣya (on Y. S. II. 32) as bearing or enduring the pairs (of opposites) such as hunger and thirst, cold and heat, standing and sitting, stillness of a stump (i. e. not indicating what is passing in one’s mind even by gestures), stillness of the body (not speaking at all), and also such observances as Kṛcchra, Cāndrāyaṇa and Sāntapana (vide H. of Dh. vol. IV. pp. 120 and 132, 134-137, 151 respectively for [[krechra|kṛcchra]] &c.).

The Vyāsabhāṣya explains ‘svādhyāya’ as the japa (inaudible repetition) of om and other holy texts or study of the śāstras treating of liberation.55 The Śat. Br. XI. 5. 7 contains a eulogy of svādhyāya and the words ‘[[svādhāyosdhyetavyah|svādhyāyo’dhyetavyaḥ]]’ (one should study the Veda) occur frequently therein. Om is the most prominent among the symbols (pratīkas) under which the upāsanā of the supra-sensible brahma was to be carried out. Vide Chān. Up. I. 1. 1 (om-ityetad-akṣaram-udgītham-upāsīta), Tai. Up. I. 8 (om-iti brahma, om-itīdaṁ sarvam), Muṇḍaka Up. II. 2. 4 (praṇavo dhanuḥ śaro hyātmā brahma tal-lakṣyam-ucyate, ‘om is the bow, the soul is the arrow, brahma is the

[[P1417]] target &c.), Praśna Up. V. 5 (Yaḥ punar-etam [[trimātrenomity- etenaivāksarena|trimātreṇomity-etenaivākṣareṇa]] param [[purusam-abhidhyāyIta|puruṣam-abhidhyāyīta]]). The Y. S. borrows this importance of Om from the Upaniṣads. The five kleśas are defined in Y. S. II. 5-9. Avidyā (ignorance)56 consists in regarding the non-eternal as eternal, the impure as pure, pain as pleasure, the non-self as the self. Asmitā (sense of personality) consists in the seer (the self) being held as if identical with the instruments of seeing (such as mind and sense organs). Abhiniveśa (clinging to life) means the craving (‘May I not cease to live, may I live’) which sweeps on by its own force and is established in this form even among the learned. [[Isvarapranidhāna|Īśvarapraṇidhāna]] has been explained above (p. 1412 note 2319). The Y. S. (II. 11 and 12) says that the kleśas have subtle conditions (viz. avidyā and asmitā) and coarse effects in the form of vṛttis (the fluctuations of the mind, rāga, dveṣa and abhiniveśa); escape from the former is secured by resolving them by means of real knowledge while the coarse ones are controlled by contemplation (dhyāna). The accumulated deposits of Karma arise from the five kleśas, are fit to be experienced in the seen birth (that is in the present birth) and in an unseen (i. e. future) birth. So long as the root (viz. the kleśas) exists the accumulated karma leads to fruition in three forms viz. birth, life (long or short) and experience, which three result in giving joy or

[[P1418]] anguish according as the accumulation is due to meritorious or evil acts. The Yogasūtra says that the Yoga system has, just like the system of medicine,57 four divisions or aspects, viz. saṁsāra (the round of births or transmigration), the cause of saṁsāra, the release from saṁsāra, the means of release (viz. samyak-darśana, insight into reality or discriminating discernment between the puruṣa and sattva, unassailed by false knowledge); sūtras 16-27 of the 2nd pāda set out these four and explain some of the terms used in defining them. Sūtra 28 provides that when impurities are washed away by practising the aṅgas of Yoga, the knowledge shines forth (more and more) till discriminatory discernment becomes perfect. Then sūtra58 29 enumerates the eight aṅgas (aids, or auxiliaries) of Yoga, viz. yama (abstinences), niyama (observances), āsana (bodily posture), prāṇāyāma (control or regulation of breath), pratyāhāra (withdrawal or suppression of the organs of sense), dhāraṇā (fixed attention), dhyāna (meditation or contemplation), samādhi (concentration or absorption into the object of meditation). The Vaikhānasasmārtasūtra enumerates the same eight

[[P1419]] angas of Yoga. The remaining sūtras of pāda two (from 30 to 55) deal with the enumeration and definitions of yamas and niyamas, their explanations and the treatment of āsana, prāṇāyāma and pratyāhāra. The Śāntiparva (304. 7 = 316. 7 of Ch. ed.) speaks of Yoga as ‘aṣṭaguṇita’ or [[astagunin|aṣṭaguṇin]]). Out of the eight aṅgas the first five are indirectly useful for samādhi, because they eradicate what are opposed to samādhi (such as hiṁsā, asatya &c.), and are therefore called bahiraṅga (external, indirect) sādhanas (means) of Yoga, while dhāraṇā, dhyāna and samādhi are said to be antaraṅga to Yoga (Y. S. III. 7, ’trayam-antaraṅgaṁ pūrvebhyaḥ’. The last three are dealt with in the 3rd pāda. It is these sūtras in the 2nd pāda that are emphasized and elaborated in Dharmaśāstra works. Therefore some detailed treatment of these matters is needed here.

In some works, such as the [[Goraksasamhitā|Gorakṣasaṁhitā]] the aṅgas of yoga are said to be only six (omitting yama and niyama or omitting some others). The Maitrāyaṇī Up. (VI. 18), the Dhyānabindu Up. the Atri-smṛti XI. 6, Dakṣa VII. 34, Skandapurāṇa, Kāśīkhaṇḍa, 41. 59 and Buddhists say the same. Manu59 provides (IV. 204) ‘a wise man should always practise yamas but not the niyamas always; one who only practises niyamas and does not practise yamas incurs sin (or falls into hell)’. This does not mean that niyamas are forbidden but it means only this that yamas are far more important than niyamas. The words ‘yama’ and ’niyama’ both occur in the Śāntiparva (cr. ed. 326. 15 and 339. 16 of Ch. ed.). Some Smṛtis omit them from the aṅgas of yoga probably because they are prescribed for all persons in general by Manu, Yāj. and others. Manu does not enumerate yamas and niyamas, but Yāj. (quoted on p. 1406, n. 2303 above) enumerates ten yamas and ten niyamas. The five yamas60 of the Y. S. are

[[P1420]] ahiṁsā (non-injury to all creatures and at all times), satya (truthfulness), asteya (non-stealing i. e. not appropriating others’ things in a way not sanctioned by the Śāstra), brahmacarya (i. e. control of the organ of generation while guarding the other organs of sense also), aparigraha (non-acceptance of anything from another beyond what is necessary for preserving the body). These five constitute for a yogin a great vrata when practised without reference to species, place, time and occasions (or exigencies). As provided by Manu, yamas have to be observed by all but there are exceptions. The observance of yamas would be called vrata, but the rigorous observance of yamas without allowing exceptions is called by the Y. S. mahāvrata which has to be observed by yogins without exception at all stages. The observance of yamas and niyamas is a first step for the soul’s ultimate success in winning Liberation or Isolation (kaivalya), since, unless the soul is purged of all sexual and egoistic desires, it cannot live the divine or spiritual life that the higher stages of yoga require. What is meant may be briefly exemplified as follows: Smṛtis recognize some exceptions to all these in the case of ordinary people. For example, it is the kṣatriya’s duty to fight and therefore it was provided by Manu (VII. 87, 89) that a kṣatriya should not run away from battle and that kṣatriyas fighting and dying in battle on both sides went to heaven. Vide Yāj. I. 324. So hiṁsā was allowed to a kṣatriya but if he wanted to follow the Yoga discipline he had to give up hiṁsā. Similarly, Smṛtis excused untruth on five occasions (vide Gautama 23. 29, Vasiṣṭha 16. 36, Ādiparva 82. 16, Śāntiparva 34. 25 and 165. 30, quoted in H. of Dh. vol. III, p. 353 and notes 536, 537). Manu in IV. 138 makes an exception for ordinary men viz. one should not speak what though true is painful to another (na brūyāt satyamapriyam). But one who enters on the discipline of Yoga must always speak the truth except when speaking the truth would result in the ruin of

[[P1421]] creatures.61 The Mitākṣarā on Yāj. III. 312 remarks that telling a falsehood in the case of arranging a marriage though excused (by smṛtis) should be avoided and also the beating of a son or a pupil as punishment by one who observes a vrata. A householder who approaches his wife on certain specified days after her monthly illness and avoids the parvan days (vide H. of Dh. vol. III. n 1425) is to be regarded as one observing brahmacarya, acc. to Yāj. I. 79 and Manu IV. 128, but if he takes to the path of Yoga, he would have to give up this latitude (vide note 2308, 233062 above) and avoid all contact whatever with all women including his own wife as the Liṅgapurāṇa emphasizes. The Yuktidīpikā, one of the earliest commentaries on the Sāṅkhyakārikā, mentions (on p. 112) five yamas but puts ‘akalkatā’ (absence of crookedness) as the 5th instead of aparigraha. The Viṣṇupurāṇa (VI. 7. 36-37) enumerates five yamas and five niyamas as in Y. S. but substitutes ‘mind bent on the highest brahma’ (kurvīta brahmaṇi tathā parasmin pravaṇaṁ manaḥ) for ‘Īśvarapraṇidhāna’. The five niyamas according to Y. S. II. 3263 are śauca (cleanliness), saṁtoṣa (contentment), tapas (austerity), svādhyāya (study of Veda), īśvarapraṇidhāna (devotion to God, or surrendering all actions to him). Three of the five niyamas, viz. tapas, svādhyāya and Īśvarapraṇidhāna constitute kriyāyoga as already stated in Y. S. II. 1. It is most difficult to give an objective definition of duty, but duties can be defined on the subjective side. The object of emphasizing duties is to make men rise above lower desires and to make the higher self shine forth. These are

[[P1422]] based more or less on the Upaniṣads; vide e. g. Chān. Up. III. 17. 4 where tapas, ahiṁsā, truthfulness, dāna, (charity) and ārjava (straightforwardness) are put forward as virtues to be cultivated by a sacrificer, Bṛ. Up. V. 2. 3, where all men are asked to cultivate dama (self-restraint), dāna (charity), dayā (kindness). Therefore, the yamas prescribed by the Yogasūtra constitute what may be called extra-regarding duties and are mostly in the nature of prohibitions (such as ‘don’t injure anyone, don’t tell a lie, don’t rob a person, don’t accept gifts’), while the niyamas are concerned with the individual himself (who has embarked on Yoga practice) and are of a positive character (viz. be clean, be contented, be austere, do study the Veda and be a devotee of Īśvara). The Amarakośa64 defines ‘yama’ as actions that are obligatory (or to be always performed) and that depend upon the body as the means for being carried out, while niyamas are acts that are not obligatory (or not to be continuously engaged in) and that depend (for carrying them out) on means that are outside the body (such as water &c). Śauca (cleanliness) is of two kinds, bāhya (of the body effected with water, loose earth, pañcagavya, pure food &c.) and ābhyantara (internal or mental). Vide H. of Dh. vol. II. pp. 651-52 and vol. IV. pp. 310-311 for details of the śauca of both kinds. A verse of Manu (V. 106) deserves to be noted65 in this connection viz. ‘of all kinds of śauca the highest is the one that relates to wealth (one must desire wealth without depriving another of his wealth by improper means); that man is śuci (pure) who is pure as to wealth and not he who is purified by loose earth and water.’ Sūtras 33-34 of pāda II provide that when the practitioner of Yoga is assailed by the outbreak of the fever of perverse thoughts (such as ‘I shall kill him who has harmed me’, ‘I shall tell a lie, I shall appropriate another’s wealth, I shall commit adultery with another’s wife’), he should revolve in his mind and cultivate the opposites of such thoughts and dwell on the consequences of such thoughts, viz. they cause endless suffering and lack of right knowledge. Yama and

[[P1423]] niyama constitute the preliminary ethical code which an aspirant for yoga must observe and some part of it must be followed by all men as Manu and Yāj. prescribe.

In sūtras 35 to 45 (of pāda II) the results of the continuous practice of the several yamas and niyamas are set out viz. when the aspirant has become established in [[ahimsā|ahiṁsā]], all beings (men and animals) give up enmity66 in his presence. When the aspirant for Yoga is well grounded in the practice of abstinence from falsehood his speech comes out to be effective unfailingly (that is, if he says to a person ‘be you a righteous man’ or ‘may you attain heaven’, the man becomes righteous or attains svarga). When he becomes rooted in abstinence from theft all [[jawels|jewels]] wait upon him from all quarters (that is, though he may not hanker after riches or [[mean8|means]], the latter come to him of their own accord). When the Yogin is firmly fixed in brahmacarya,67 he [[securos|secures]] energy (whereby he treads the path to such supernormal powers as [[animā|aṇimā]]) and when he reaches perfection he is able to transfer knowledge about Yoga and its [[angas|aṅgas]] to his disciples. In [[Y, S. L|Y. S. I.]] 20 it is stated that [[asamprajñāta-samadhi|asamprajñāta-samādhi]] follows when the yogin has faith, [[virya|vīrya]] (energy) and other qualities.
